&-\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}\bigg[\frac{1-y_{IJk}}{1+y_{IJk}}U_{1}(y_{IJk})+2\frac{1+\alpha_{IJ}^{2}}{1-\alpha_{IJ}^{2}}\log(y_{IJk})\log(\alpha_{IJ})\bigg]\\
&+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\bigg[\frac{1+\alpha_{IJ}^{2}}{1-\alpha_{IJ}^{2}}\log(y_{IJk})\bigg(2V_{1}(\alpha_{IJ})+M_{100}(\alpha_{IJ})\bigg)\\
&\hskip 28.45274pt-\frac{1}{2}\frac{1-y_{IJk}}{1+y_{IJk}}U_{2}(\alpha_{IJ},y_{IJk})+2\log\left(y_{IJk}\right)\log^{2}\left(\alpha_{IJ}\right)\\
&\hskip 28.45274pt-\frac{1}{3}\log^{3}\left(y_{IJk}\right)+\frac{7}{6}\pi^{2}\log\left(y_{IJk}\right)\bigg]\bigg\}+{\cal O}(\epsilon^{0})+{\cal O}(\lambda),\end{split}

(149)

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}\lambda^{-2\epsilon}{\mathbb{R}}_{(IJk)}^{(-2)}+\lambda^{-\epsilon}{\mathbb{R}}_{(IJk)}^{(-1)}=&\left(\frac{\alpha_{s}}{4\pi}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{\bar{m}^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\right)^{-2\epsilon}\bigg\{-\frac{1}{\epsilon^{3}}\log(y_{IJk})\\
&+\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}\bigg[\frac{1-y_{IJk}}{1+y_{IJk}}U_{1}(y_{IJk})+2\frac{1+\alpha_{IJ}^{2}}{1-\alpha_{IJ}^{2}}\log(y_{IJk})\log(\alpha_{IJ})\bigg]\\
&+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\bigg[-\frac{1+\alpha_{IJ}^{2}}{1-\alpha_{IJ}^{2}}\log(y_{IJk})\bigg(V_{1}(\alpha_{IJ})+2\log(\lambda)\log(\alpha_{IJ})\bigg)\\
&+\frac{1}{2}\frac{1-y_{IJk}}{1+y_{IJk}}U_{2}(\alpha_{IJ},y_{IJk})+\log^{2}(\lambda)\log\left(y_{IJk}\right)\\
&+\frac{1}{12}\log^{3}\left(y_{IJk}\right)-\frac{1}{2}\pi^{2}\log\left(y_{IJk}\right)\bigg]\bigg\}+{\cal O}(\epsilon^{0})+{\cal O}(\lambda).\end{split}

(150)

The expansion of the correlator is then

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left(\frac{\alpha_{s}}{4\pi}\right)^{2}{\cal T}_{\lambda}\left[w^{(2)}\right]=\,\,&\sum_{I<J<K}\mathbf{T}_{IJK}{\cal T}_{\lambda}\left[{\mathcal{Y}}_{(IJk)}^{(2)}+{\mathcal{Y}}_{(IJ)(IK)}^{(2)}+{\mathcal{Y}}_{(JK)(JI)}^{(2)}+{\mathcal{Y}}_{(KI)(KJ)}^{(2)}\right]+\cdots\\
=\,\,&\sum_{I<J}\mathbf{T}_{IJk}\left(\frac{\alpha_{s}}{4\pi}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{\bar{m}^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\right)^{-2\epsilon}\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log(y_{IJk})\\
&\bigg\{\frac{1+\alpha_{IJ}^{2}}{1-\alpha_{IJ}^{2}}\bigg[V_{1}(\alpha_{IJ})+M_{100}(\alpha_{IJ})-2\log(\lambda)\log(\alpha_{IJ})\bigg]\\
&+\log^{2}(\lambda)+2\log^{2}\left(\alpha_{IJ}\right)-\frac{1}{4}\log^{2}\left(y_{IJk}\right)+\frac{2}{3}\pi^{2}\bigg\}+{\cal O}(\epsilon^{0})+{\cal O}(\lambda).\\
&+\cdots.\end{split}

(151)

As expected, all triple and double poles in
\epsilon
cancel in the sum of eqs. (
149
) and (
150
). Notice that there is still
\lambda
dependence even at the correlator level. We shall verify below that it is eliminated upon extracting the soft AD.

We comment that
{\cal T}_{\lambda}\left[w^{(2)}\right]
in eq. (
151
) does have double poles in
\epsilon
in colour dipole terms, which we have not written explicitly. These double poles are related exclusively to the running coupling
Gardi:2011yz
, and are proportional to
b_{0}
of eq. (
5
),

\displaystyle{\cal T}_{\lambda}\left[w^{(2,-2)}\right]=-\frac{b_{0}}{2}{\cal T}_{\lambda}\left[w^{(1,-1)}\right].

(152)

Focusing here on the tripole colour structure which is free of
b_{0}
, it will be convenient to simply set
b_{0}=0
in the remainder of this section, so the leading order of
{\cal T}_{\lambda}\left[w^{(2)}\right]
is single pole in
\epsilon
, as obtained eq. (
151
).

We now split the two-loop correlator
w^{(2)}
into the separate hard and IR region contributions, following eq. (
79
),

{\cal T}_{\lambda}\left[w^{(2)}\right]=h^{(2)}+r^{(2)}\,,

(153)

Both
h^{(2)}
and
r^{(2)}
contain
\mathbf{T}_{IJk}
, as well as other colour structures which we represent by ellipsis:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left(\frac{\alpha_{s}}{4\pi}\right)^{2}h^{(2)}=\,\,&\sum_{I<J}\left(\frac{\alpha_{s}}{4\pi}\right)^{2}h^{(2)}_{(IJk)}+\cdots\,,\end{split}

(154a)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left(\frac{\alpha_{s}}{4\pi}\right)^{2}r^{(2)}=\,\,&\sum_{I<J}\left(\frac{\alpha_{s}}{4\pi}\right)^{2}r^{(2)}_{(IJk)}+\cdots\,,\end{split}

(154b)

The tripole-term components may be written in terms of region functions as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{split}h^{(2)}_{(IJk)}=\textbf{T}_{IJk}{\mathbb{R}}_{(IJk)}^{(0)}\end{split}

(155a)

\displaystyle\begin{split}r^{(2)}_{(IJk)}=\textbf{T}_{IJk}\left[\lambda^{-2\epsilon}{\mathbb{R}}_{(IJk)}^{(-2)}+\lambda^{-\epsilon}{\mathbb{R}}_{(IJk)}^{(-1)}\right].\end{split}

(155b)

The kinematic functions in
h_{(IJk)}^{(2)}
and
r_{(IJk)}^{(2)}
are exactly given in eq. (
149
) and (
150
), respectively.

Using eq. (
22b
), the soft AD is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{1}{\epsilon}\Gamma_{\mathrm{UV}}^{(2)}=\,\,&\frac{1}{\epsilon}{\cal T}_{\lambda}\left[-4w^{(2,-1)}-2\left[w^{(1,-1)},w^{(1,0)}\right]\right]\\
=\,\,&-\frac{4}{\epsilon}\left(h^{(2,-1)}+r^{(2,-1)}\right)-\frac{2}{\epsilon}\left[h^{(1,-1)}+r^{(1,-1)},h^{(1,0)}+r^{(1,0)}\right]\\
=\,\,&\sing\left(\,\,-4\left(h^{(2)}+r^{(2)}\right)-2\left[\sing\left(h^{(1)}+r^{(1)}\right),\,\reg\left(h^{(1)}+r^{(1)}\right)\right]\right)\,,\end{split}

(156)

where we have omitted the trivial combinations
h^{(2,-3)}+r^{(2,-3)}
,
h^{(2,-2)}+r^{(2,-2)}
, and
h^{(1,-2)}+r^{(1,-2)}
in the second line.
The operators
\sing
and
\reg
are defined in eq. (
88
), and the one-loop result can be found in eqs. (
83
)
and (
85
). Upon substituting these one-loop expressions into eq. (
156
), and using
eq. (
24
) to obtain a tripole colour structure from the commutator, we finds that the
\log(\lambda)
terms appearing in the two-loop expressions cancel by the commutator term, and the soft AD
\Omega_{(IJk)}
of eq. (
29
) is exactly reproduced.

4.3
Neutral modes

Among the modes collected in table
2
, only three survive at the correlator level, these are the hard mode
H
, the IR-collinear mode
C_{\mathrm{IR}}
and the neutral collinear mode
C_{N}
; these are the relevant modes at both one and two loops, see eqs. (
78
) and (
148
). The first two are also ubiquitous in the on-shell expansion of wide-angle scattering amplitudes. However, the neutral collinear mode
C_{N}
, which features both large and small momentum components, is special to the expansion of correlators of Wilson lines.
In this section, we claim that the presence of this second neutral mode, alongside the hard mode, is a consequence of the rescaling symmetry of semi-infinite Wilson lines.

4.3.1
Rescaling symmetry and the complementary lightcone expansion

The momentum-space analysis in section
4.1
at one loop, and in sections
4.2.1
and
4.2.2
at two loops, was performed in a special frame
O
where
\beta_{k}=(\beta_{k}^{+},0,0)
, with the opposite lightlike direction being
\bar{\beta}_{k}=(0,\bar{\beta}_{k}^{-},0)
. In this frame, the timelike velocity
\beta_{K}
behave as
\beta_{K}\sim(1,\lambda^{2},\lambda)
; it has a large “plus” momentum component, and thus is close to the lightlike direction defined by
\beta_{k}
. The other two timelike velocities are hard, i.e.,
\beta_{I}\sim\beta_{J}\sim(1,1,1)
. The scalar products behave as

\displaystyle\beta_{K}^{2}\sim{\cal O}(\lambda^{2})\,,\qquad\beta_{K}\cdot\beta_{I}\sim\beta_{K}\cdot\beta_{J}\sim\beta_{I}\cdot\beta_{J}\sim\beta_{I}^{2}\sim\beta_{J}^{2}\sim{\cal O}(\lambda^{0})\,.

(157)

Because of the rescaling symmetry, the kinematic variables entering the correlator are the three scalar products of the normalized velocities,

\displaystyle v_{K}\cdot v_{I}\sim v_{K}\cdot v_{J}\sim{\cal O}(\lambda^{-1})\,,\qquad v_{I}\cdot v_{J}\sim{\cal O}(\lambda^{0})\,,

(158)

where
v_{I}=\beta_{I}/\sqrt{\beta_{I}^{2}}
.
The scaling law of the normalized velocities in frame
O
is given by

\displaystyle v_{K}\sim(\lambda^{-1},\lambda,1)\qquad v_{I}\sim v_{J}\sim(1,1,1).

(159)

One can of course consider other Lorentz frames, in which the scaling law of the velocities would differ from (
159
), without modifying the invariants (
158
).

Consider specifically a Lorentz transformation to
the frame
O^{\prime}
which is related to the frame
O
through the boost
B_{3}\left(\eta\right)
along the third
14
14

14

The third spatial direction is chosen as the same as that of the spacial momentum of
\beta_{k}=(\beta_{k}^{+},0,0)
. The lightcone coordinates are defined as

\beta_{k}^{+}=\frac{\beta_{k}^{0}+\beta_{k}^{3}}{\sqrt{2}}\,,\qquad\beta_{k}^{-}=\frac{\beta_{k}^{0}-\beta_{k}^{3}}{\sqrt{2}}=0\,.

spatial direction with rapidity
\eta=\log(\lambda)
. The normalized velocities then become

\displaystyle\begin{split}v_{K}\sim(\lambda^{-1},\lambda,1)&\xrightarrow{\;B_{3}\left(\log(\lambda)\right)\;}v_{K}\sim(1,1,1)\,,\end{split}

(160a)

\displaystyle\begin{split}v_{I}\sim(1,1,1)&\xrightarrow{\;B_{3}\left(\log(\lambda)\right)\;}v_{I}\sim(\lambda,\lambda^{-1},1)\,,\end{split}

(160b)

\displaystyle\begin{split}v_{J}\sim(1,1,1)&\xrightarrow{\;B_{3}\left(\log(\lambda)\right)\;}v_{J}\sim(\lambda,\lambda^{-1},1)\,.\end{split}

(160c)

We observe that the behaviour of the velocities in the frame
O^{\prime}
can also be obtained from a
different physical limit
where
\beta_{K}
remains generic,
\beta_{K}\sim(1,1,1)
, while
the other two external velocities,
\beta_{I}
and
\beta_{J}
, become collinear to the opposite lightlike direction defined by
\bar{\beta}_{k}
, i.e.,
\beta_{I}\sim\beta_{J}\sim(\lambda^{2},1,\lambda)
. The Lorentz invariants constructed by ordinary velocities behave as

\displaystyle\beta_{K}^{2}\sim\beta_{K}\cdot\beta_{J}\sim\beta_{K}\cdot\beta_{I}\sim{\cal O}(\lambda^{0})\,,\qquad\beta_{I}^{2}\sim\beta_{J}^{2}\sim\beta_{I}\cdot\beta_{J}\sim{\cal O}(\lambda^{2})\,,

(161)

which is a completely different expansion compared with that defined in eq. (
157
). In the extreme limit eq. (
161
) describes two collinear massless particles
I
and
J
interacting with one massive particle
K
(plus any number of non-coloured particles, sharing the recoil momentum). This can be contrasted to the original limit (
157
), where a single massless particle
K
is interacting with two massive particles,
I
and
J
, scattering at generic angles (plus any number of non-coloured particles).

This puzzling situation is explained by the fact the behaviour of the scalar products of the normalized velocities (
158
), is consistent with both the original limit we considered, eq. (
157
), and the one we formulated in eq. (
161
). In other words, knowing only (
158
) one cannot distinguish between the two physical situations.
This can be most easily understood by re-expressing (
158
) back in terms non-normalized velocities, namely

\displaystyle\frac{\beta_{K}\cdot\beta_{I}}{\sqrt{\beta_{K}^{2}\,\beta_{I}^{2}}}\sim\frac{\beta_{K}\cdot\beta_{J}}{\sqrt{\beta_{K}^{2}\,\beta_{J}^{2}}}\sim{\cal O}(\lambda^{-1})\,,\qquad\frac{\beta_{I}\cdot\beta_{J}}{\sqrt{\beta_{I}^{2}\,\beta_{J}^{2}}}\sim{\cal O}(\lambda^{0})\,.

(162)

It straightforward to check that either the limit of eq. (
157
) or that in eq. (
161
) can be consistent with eq. (
162
).

Referring to the original expansion in eq. (
157
) as the
lightcone expansion
, the new expansion defined in eq. (
161
), may be called the
complementary lightcone expansion
, referring to the fact that the complementary set of Wilson lines (or particles) to line
K
, are now highly boosted in the “minus” direction.
Although these are two distinct limits, because of the rescaling symmetry, the integrals before the expansion really depend only on the scalar products of normalized velocities. Therefore, the two expansions, while having different physical interpretations, are described by the very same result.

Let us point out another instance where the same phenomenon was observed
Duhr:2025cye
;
GZ-PRL
;
GZ-TBP
.
This is the case of the soft AD of four coloured particles, one of which (
Q
) is heavy and the rest (
i
,
j
and
k
) are massless. This object depends on only three rescaling invariant cross ratios
Liu:2022elt
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}r_{ijQ}&=\frac{(\beta_{i}\cdot\beta_{j})\beta_{Q}^{2}}{(\beta_{i}\cdot\beta_{Q})\,\,(\beta_{j}\cdot\beta_{Q})}\,,\\
r_{ikQ}&=\frac{(\beta_{i}\cdot\beta_{k})\beta_{Q}^{2}}{(\beta_{i}\cdot\beta_{Q})\,\,(\beta_{k}\cdot\beta_{Q})}\,,\\
r_{jkQ}&=\frac{(\beta_{j}\cdot\beta_{k})\beta_{Q}^{2}}{(\beta_{j}\cdot\beta_{Q})\,\,(\beta_{k}\cdot\beta_{Q})}\,.\end{split}

(163)

As a consequence of the rescaling symmetry with respect to
\beta_{Q}
, there are two physically-distinct limits of the velocities, which correspond to the very same limit of the cross ratios in eq. (
163
), namely
r_{ijQ}\sim r_{ikQ}\sim r_{jkQ}\sim\lambda
, with the ratios between them remaining finite for
\lambda\to 0
. The two limits are the massless limit:

\displaystyle\beta_{Q}^{2}\sim\lambda,\qquad\beta_{i}\cdot\beta_{j}\sim\beta_{i}\cdot\beta_{k}\sim\beta_{j}\cdot\beta_{k}\sim{\cal O}(\lambda^{0})\,,

(164)

and the triple collinear limit:

\displaystyle\beta_{Q}^{2}\sim{\cal O}(\lambda^{0}),\qquad\beta_{i}\cdot\beta_{j}\sim\beta_{i}\cdot\beta_{k}\sim\beta_{j}\cdot\beta_{k}\sim\lambda\,,

(165)

where in either of these cases, one considers

\displaystyle\beta_{i}\cdot\beta_{Q}\sim\beta_{j}\cdot\beta_{Q}\sim\beta_{k}\cdot\beta_{Q}\sim{\cal O}(\lambda^{0})\,.

(166)

These two limits represent, respectively, the lightcone expansion of particle
Q
, and the complementary lightcone expansion, where all other particles become collinear. We stress that this is true in the presence of an arbitrary momentum recoil, so this is a non-trivial relation two between physically distinct limits of the anomalous dimension, which coincide because of the rescaling symmetry.
The considerations above indicate that this is a general feature.

4.3.2
Degeneracy of neutral modes

Let us return to our original set up of three Wilson lines, one of which is becoming lightlike, and turn to discuss the workings of the MoR. We observe that although the relevant kinematic invariants in eq. (
161
) are the same in the two expansions,
the loop-momentum modes are different
. To see this, recall that the two expansions are naturally described in different frames, as we have seen for the velocity components in eq. (
160
). Consider now the three loop-momentum modes appearing in the expansion of the correlator,
H
,
C_{\mathrm{IR}}
and
C_{N}
. In the frame
O^{\prime}
, they become, respectively,

\displaystyle\begin{split}H\sim(1,1,1)&\xrightarrow{\;B_{3}\left(\log(\lambda)\right)\;}\bar{C}_{N}\sim(\lambda,\lambda^{-1},1)\,,\end{split}

(167a)

\displaystyle\begin{split}C_{\mathrm{IR}}\sim(1,\lambda,\sqrt{\lambda})&\xrightarrow{\;B_{3}\left(\log(\lambda)\right)\;}\bar{C}_{\mathrm{IR}}\sim(\lambda,1,\sqrt{\lambda})\,,\end{split}

(167b)

\displaystyle C_{N}\sim(\lambda^{-1},\lambda,1)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\;B_{3}\left(\log(\lambda)\right)\;}H\sim(1,1,1)\,.

(167c)

Notice that the special neutral mode
C_{N}
in frame
O
has become the hard mode in
O^{\prime}
, which is associated with the strict limit of the complementary lightcone expansion. The two types of neutral modes behave as the hard mode in the two different frames, respectively.

With the modes identified in frame
O^{\prime}
, the complementary lightlike expansion is, for example, at one loop,

\displaystyle{\cal T}_{\lambda}\left[{\mathcal{Y}}_{JK}\right]={\cal R}_{(j_{\bar{k}}K)}^{\{\bar{C}_{N}\}}+{\cal R}_{(j_{\bar{k}}K)}^{\{H\}}+\lambda^{-\epsilon}{\cal R}_{(j_{\bar{k}}K)}^{\{\bar{C}_{\mathrm{IR}}\}}\,,

(168)

where the subscript
J
has been replaced by
j_{\bar{k}}
, identifying the limit where
v_{J}
is highly boosted in the direction collinear to
\bar{\beta}_{k}
, as in (
160c
) with
\lambda\to 0
.
Because the region functions are Lorentz invariant, eq. (
167
) implies a direct relation between the two expansions:

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\cal R}_{(j_{\bar{k}}K)}^{\{\bar{C}_{N}\}}={\cal R}_{(Jk)}^{\{H\}}\,,\end{split}

(169a)

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\cal R}_{(j_{\bar{k}}K)}^{\{\bar{C}_{\mathrm{IR}}\}}={\cal R}_{(Jk)}^{\{C_{\mathrm{IR}}\}}\,,\end{split}

(169b)

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\cal R}_{(j_{\bar{k}}K)}^{\{H\}}={\cal R}_{(Jk)}^{\{C_{N}\}}\,.\end{split}

(169c)

In the remaining of this section, we will always use the notations in frame
O
.

We stress that the strict limits of the two expansions are different. At one loop, they are accidentally the same, see the result of
{\cal R}_{(Jk)}^{\{H\}}
and
{\cal R}_{(Jk)}^{\{C_{N}\}}
in eqs. (
217
) and (
219
), respectively.
At two loops the situation is entirely different. While hard region of the original lightcone expansion,
{\cal R}_{(IJk)}^{\{H,H\}}
, is a non-trivial function given in eq. (
292
), the hard region of the complementary lightcone expansion,
{\cal R}_{(IJk)}^{\{C_{N},C_{N}\}}
, is vanishing.

In fact, the
\{C_{N},C_{N}\}
region vanishes for each web separately, as reported in eqs. (
107
) and (
138
) above.
Let us now explain, as an example, the reason why the contribution of this region is vanishing for the
connected web, i.e., why

{\mathcal{Y}}_{(IJk)}^{\{C_{N},C_{N}\}}=0\,.

In frame
O
, the
C_{N}
mode, characterized by a large
q^{+}
, extracts only the “minus” lightcone component of the timelike velocity in the denominator of the eikonal propagator,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{-v\cdot q-1+i\varepsilon}\xrightarrow{\;\text{mode}_{q}=C_{N}\;}\frac{\lambda}{-v^{-}q^{+}+i\varepsilon}+O(\lambda^{2})\equiv\frac{\lambda}{-\kappa\bar{\beta}_{k}\cdot q+i\varepsilon}+O(\lambda^{2}),

(170)

where
\kappa
is the ratio of
v^{-}
and
\bar{\beta}_{k}^{-}
. Here the timelike velocity
v\sim(1,1,1)
can be either
v_{I}
or
v_{J}
.
Equation (
170
) leads to a simplification of the integrand. In particular, the integrand
Y_{(IJK)}
defined in eq. (
94
) becomes

\displaystyle\begin{split}Y_{(IJK)}\xrightarrow{\;\left\{C_{N},C_{N}\right\}\;}\,\,&\frac{\lambda^{2}}{\kappa_{I}\kappa_{J}}\,\frac{1}{q_{I}^{2}+i\varepsilon}\frac{1}{q_{J}^{2}+i\varepsilon}\frac{1}{q_{K}^{2}+i\varepsilon}\\
&\times\left(\frac{1}{-\bar{\beta}_{k}\cdot q_{I}+i\varepsilon}\right)\left(\frac{1}{-\bar{\beta}_{k}\cdot q_{J}+i\varepsilon}\right)\left(\frac{1}{-v_{K}\cdot q_{K}-1+i\varepsilon}\right)+O(\lambda^{3})\,.\end{split}

(171)

Having factorized
\kappa_{I}\kappa_{J}
, the leading term integrates to a constant, and vanishes upon including the
(I,J)
antisymmetric numerator.
Finally, we note that eq. (
171
) is consistent with directly applying the complementary lightcone expansion on
Y_{(IJK)}
.

To conclude, we have shown that the two neutral modes can be interpreted, respectively, as the hard modes of two physically-distinct expansions. We have learnt that the reason these two expansions are linked, is that the integrals only depends on a subset of the kinematic invariants, namely the rescaling-invariant subset.
Rescaling symmetry therefore provides the fundamental reason for the degeneracy of the neutral modes, which was observed already in the Lorentz-invariant parameters-space analysis.

5
Renormalization of correlators with timelike and lightlike Wilson lines

In the MoR, the leading-power term of the hard region has a special status as the strict limit, taken at integrand level. In the context of our lightcone expansion of Wilson-line correlators one can therefore view the (leading power term of the) hard region as the computation of a mixed correlator, involving both timelike and lightlike lines.

For correlators of timelike Wilson lines in a complete regularization scheme, multiplicative renormalizability enables us to extract the soft AD from the computation of
the
1/\epsilon
pole. However, as we have seen in section
4
, the hard region of the mixed tripole term
\mathbb{R}_{(IJk)}^{(0)}
, where the
k
line is strictly lightlike, contains a third-order pole in
\epsilon
, see eq. (
149
). Since the tripole is a new colour structure appearing first at two loops, such higher-order poles cannot be attributed to the short-distance renormalization of the strong coupling – nor any operator – and hence they immediately violate the multiplicative renormalizability of the correlator.

Fortunately, using MoR, the origin of these extra poles becomes clear, with the modes as well as the regions being classified as IR, neutral, and UV according to their scaling exponent
n_{R}
of eq. (
48
). This classification tells us the characteristic loop-momentum virtuality giving rise to the various singularities.
We will now use this information in order to disentangle between short- and long-distance effects in a correltor containing strictly lightlike Wilson lines.

Figure 8
:
Configuration-space sketch of different modes and the corresponding renormalization of the mixed correlator involving timelike (thick) and lightlike (thin) Wilson lines. The modes are colour-coded following the conventions of table
2
. In particular, the connected tripole is in the
\{H,C_{\mathrm{IR}}\}
region (one loop is hard and the second is IR-collinear), while the single gluon exchange in green is in a neutral collinear (
C_{N}
) region. In turn, the red gluon represents a long-distance (soft) exchange that is removed by the IR regulator, while the blue gluon represents a genuine contribution of short-distance origin to the soft AD. The two renormalization scales
{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mu_{\mathrm{IR}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.4527,0.132,0.4669}{\mu_{\mathrm{UV}}}}
are represented by dashed lines, while the regulator
\bar{m}
is represented by the full line in between the two.

Let us recall the region structure we obtained at one and two loops in section
4
. At the correlator level UV regions do not contribute (see eq. (
147
)), leaving behind only neutral
and IR regions. Furthermore, neutral regions other than the hard only contribute to the dipole colour structure, not to the tripole.
15
15

15

The neutral regions correspond contributions that may not be uniquely identified as either of short or long-distance origin; we will return to discuss non-hard neutral regions later on in this section.

As a result, a simple picture emerges: tripole singularities arise from just two sources, the hard region, (i.e. the strict limit) of eq. (
149
), and IR regions, eq. (
150
), representing contributions of long-distance origin. It is natural to assume that this broad classification of the origin of singularities applies in general in the lightcone expansion, although here we will only study it at two loops.

Based on the region analysis, to “renormalize” the mixed timelike-lightlike correlator, it is necessary to consider an additional “renormalization” procedure at infinity in configuration space, where IR singularities are generated; this is illustrated in figure
8
along with some of the relevant regions.
We put the word renormalization in quotation marks to emphasise that this operation does not correspond to short-distance renormalization of any operator. Rather, it is similar to removing long-distance singularities from on-shell scattering amplitudes, see e.g.
Catani:1998bh
;
Aybat:2006wq
;
Becher:2009qa
;
Gardi:2009qi
;
Gardi:2009zv
.

We thus propose the following conjecture for the singularity structure of the mixed correlator:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\left<\phi_{\beta_{1}}\cdots\phi_{\beta_{N}}\Phi^{(m)}_{\beta_{N+1}}\!\cdots\Phi^{(m)}_{\beta_{N+M}}\right>_{\text{ren.}({\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mu_{\mathrm{IR}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.4527,0.132,0.4669}{\mu_{\mathrm{UV}}}})}\\
&\hskip 100.0pt=\,\,Z_{\mathrm{IR}}({\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mu_{\mathrm{IR}}}})\left<\phi_{\beta_{1}}\cdots\phi_{\beta_{N}}\Phi^{(m)}_{\beta_{N+1}}\!\cdots\Phi^{(m)}_{\beta_{N+M}}\right>Z_{\mathrm{UV}}({\color[rgb]{0.4527,0.132,0.4669}{\mu_{\mathrm{UV}}}})\,,\end{split}

(172)

with the renormalization factors taking the form

\displaystyle\begin{split}Z_{\mathrm{UV}}({\color[rgb]{0.4527,0.132,0.4669}{\mu_{\mathrm{UV}}}})&\equiv\text{P}\exp\left[\int_{{\color[rgb]{0.4527,0.132,0.4669}{\mu_{\mathrm{UV}}}}}^{\infty}\frac{d\tau}{\tau}\Gamma_{\mathrm{UV}}\right]\,,\end{split}

(173a)

\displaystyle\begin{split}Z_{\mathrm{IR}}({\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mu_{\mathrm{IR}}}})&\equiv\text{P}\exp\left[\int_{0}^{{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mu_{\mathrm{IR}}}}}\frac{d\tau}{\tau}\Gamma_{\mathrm{IR}}\right]\,,\end{split}

(173b)

where
{\color[rgb]{0.4527,0.132,0.4669}{\mu_{\mathrm{UV}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mu_{\mathrm{IR}}}}
serve as two separate factorization scales, such that
{\color[rgb]{0.4527,0.132,0.4669}{\mu_{\mathrm{UV}}}}\geq\bar{m}\geq{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mu_{\mathrm{IR}}}}
.

Figure 9
:
The factorization structure of eq. (
172
) for a correlator consisting of one lightlike Wilson line and two timelike ones. The attachments along the Wilson lines are consistent with the colour ordering prescribed there. The indices
i_{n}
,
j_{n}
and
k_{n}
represent colour indices in the representation of each of the corresponding Wilson lines; these indices are summed over when the matrices are multiplied. Note that
Z_{\mathrm{IR}}
would be trivial if the line
k
were taken to be massive, leading to the one-sided factorization of figure
2
.

The general structure of the renormalization in eq. (
172
) is sketched in figure
9
in configuration space.
In analogy with the treatment of singularities in on-shell amplitudes, the renormalization process of the mixed correlator consists of two separate steps, one in which short-distance singularities are compensated for by
Z_{\mathrm{UV}}
and a second where the remaining, long-distance ones are removed by
Z_{\mathrm{IR}}
.
16
16

16

The difference to the amplitude case is of course that here both operations concern the correlator itself. Of course, additional renormalization of the coupling is assumed here, but will not be discussed explicitly.

Conceptually, these operate at two different cutoff scales
{\color[rgb]{0.4527,0.132,0.4669}{\mu_{\mathrm{UV}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mu_{\mathrm{IR}}}}
. Of course, in practice these two scales can identified as a single renormalization point
\mu
. We keep them distinct in order to clearly demonstrate that the two renormalization procedures are independent.
Note that the (path) ordering in eq. (
173
) is such that the contributions of high virtulaity are always placed to the right of those of lower virtuality. This is in line with the corresponding renormalization-group equations:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{d}{d\log{\color[rgb]{0.4527,0.132,0.4669}{\mu_{\mathrm{UV}}}}}Z_{\mathrm{UV}}({\color[rgb]{0.4527,0.132,0.4669}{\mu_{\mathrm{UV}}}})=\,\,&-\Gamma_{\mathrm{UV}}(\alpha_{s}({\color[rgb]{0.4527,0.132,0.4669}{\mu_{\mathrm{UV}}}}))\,Z_{\mathrm{UV}}({\color[rgb]{0.4527,0.132,0.4669}{\mu_{\mathrm{UV}}}}),\end{split}

(174a)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{d}{d\log{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mu_{\mathrm{IR}}}}}Z_{\mathrm{IR}}({\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mu_{\mathrm{IR}}}})=\,\,&Z_{\mathrm{IR}}({\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mu_{\mathrm{IR}}}})\,\Gamma_{\mathrm{IR}}({\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mu_{\mathrm{IR}}}},\alpha_{s}({\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mu_{\mathrm{IR}}}}))\,.\end{split}

(174b)

On the right-hand side of eq. (
172
), the mixed correlator (involving both timelike and lightlike lines) is just the hard region. To remove all its singularities we multiply it from the right by
Z_{\mathrm{UV}}
, and from the left by
Z_{\mathrm{IR}}
.
Because the UV regions cancel (at the correlator level), the UV singularities of the mixed correlator, which are compensated by
Z_{\mathrm{UV}}
, directly correspond to the soft AD, and
\Gamma_{\mathrm{UV}}
is thus the same as that given in eq. (
30
).
Z_{\mathrm{UV}}
is the proper short-distance renormalization factor which can be computed from the correlator of timelike Wilson lines, as in section
2
. Note that
\Gamma_{\mathrm{UV}}
therefore depends on the scale only via the argument of the coupling,
\alpha_{s}
, as shown in eq. (
174a
).
In turn, the factor
Z_{\mathrm{IR}}
in eq. (
172
) encodes IR singularities; its role is to remove all the extra long-distance singularities that have been generated in the hard region by taking the Wilson lines to be strictly lightlike at integrand level.
In line with this, its dependence on the scale is not only via the argument of the coupling, but also explicit, as in eq. (
174b
), reflecting the presence of overlapping singularities.

With the region functions computed, we have enough information to determine
\Gamma_{\mathrm{IR}}
for the tripole colour structure. Let us proceed by rewriting the right-hand side of eq. (
172
) as exponentiated perturbative expansions of each factor. Firstly, we construct the expansion of the yet unknown
Z_{\mathrm{IR}}
as

\displaystyle\begin{split}Z_{\mathrm{IR}}({\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mu_{\mathrm{IR}}}})=\,\,&\exp\left\{\sum_{n}\left[\frac{\alpha_{s}(\bar{m})}{4\pi}\right]^{n}\left(\frac{\bar{m}^{2}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mu}}}{}^{2}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mathrm{IR}}}}}}\right)^{n\epsilon}\zeta^{(n)}\right\}\\
=\,\,&\exp\left\{\sum_{n}\left[\frac{\alpha_{s}(\bar{m})}{4\pi}\right]^{n}\left(\frac{\bar{m}^{2}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mu}}}{}^{2}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mathrm{IR}}}}}}\right)^{n\epsilon}\sum_{l<0}\zeta^{(n,l)}\epsilon^{l}\right\}\,.\end{split}

(175)

Assuming minimal subtraction,
\zeta^{(n)}
only contains negative powers of
\epsilon
, i.e.,
l<0
. Notice that the strong coupling
\alpha_{s}
is evaluated at the scale
\bar{m}
instead of
{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mu_{\mathrm{IR}}}}
, and the evolution of
\alpha_{s}
, controlled by eq. (
4
), generates the additional factor
\left(\frac{\bar{m}^{2}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mu}}}{}^{2}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mathrm{IR}}}}}}\right)^{n\epsilon}
. To simplify the derivation, we have set
b_{0}=0
. The same setup for the strong coupling will be used for
\Gamma_{\mathrm{UV}}
below.

Next, the mixed correlator is the strict lightlike limit of the correlator with respect to
\beta_{i}^{2}
for
i=1,\ldots,N
, the so-called hard region,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left<\phi_{\beta_{1}}\cdots\phi_{\beta_{N}}\Phi^{(m)}_{\beta_{N+1}}\cdots\Phi^{(m)}_{\beta_{N+M}}\right>=\,\,&\exp\left\{\sum_{n}\left[\frac{\alpha_{s}(\bar{m})}{4\pi}\right]^{n}h^{(n)}\right\}\\
=\,\,&\exp\left\{\sum_{n}\left[\frac{\alpha_{s}(\bar{m})}{4\pi}\right]^{n}\sum_{l}h^{(n,l)}\epsilon^{l}\right\}\,,\end{split}

(176)

Here of course both negative and positive powers of
\epsilon
appear.
Finally, the UV renormalization takes a similar form of that in eq. (
21
),

\displaystyle\begin{split}Z_{\mathrm{UV}}({\color[rgb]{0.4527,0.132,0.4669}{\mu_{\mathrm{UV}}}})=\,\,&\exp\left\{\frac{\alpha_{s}(\bar{m})}{4\pi}\left(\frac{\bar{m}^{2}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.4527,0.132,0.4669}{\mu}}}{}^{2}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.4527,0.132,0.4669}{\mathrm{UV}}}}}}\right)^{\epsilon}\frac{1}{2\epsilon}\Gamma_{\mathrm{UV}}^{(1)}+\left[\frac{\alpha_{s}(\bar{m})}{4\pi}\right]^{2}\left(\frac{\bar{m}^{2}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.4527,0.132,0.4669}{\mu}}}{}^{2}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.4527,0.132,0.4669}{\mathrm{UV}}}}}}\right)^{2\epsilon}\frac{1}{4\epsilon}\Gamma_{\mathrm{UV}}^{(2)}+{\cal O}(\alpha_{s}^{3})\right\}\,,\end{split}

(177)

except that we set
b_{0}=0
for simplicity.
The first two orders,
\Gamma_{\mathrm{UV}}^{(1)}
and
\Gamma_{\mathrm{UV}}^{(2)}
, written in terms of
h^{(n)}
and
r^{(n)}
, have been presented in eqs. (
89
) and (
156
), respectively.

Using the expressions given in eqs. (
176
), (
89
) and (
156
),
\zeta^{(1)}
and
\zeta^{(2)}
of eq. (
175
) can be obtained by requiring the left-hand-side of eq. (
172
) is finite.
More specifically, by expanding the product of the three non-Abelian exponentials on the right-hand side of eq. (
172
), and then extracting the singularities, we have, at one loop,

\displaystyle\begin{split}0=\sing\left[\left(\frac{\bar{m}^{2}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mu}}}{}^{2}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mathrm{IR}}}}}}\right)^{\epsilon}\zeta^{(1)}+h^{(1)}+\left(\frac{\bar{m}^{2}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.4527,0.132,0.4669}{\mu}}}{}^{2}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.4527,0.132,0.4669}{\mathrm{UV}}}}}}\right)^{\epsilon}\frac{1}{2\epsilon}\Gamma_{\mathrm{UV}}^{(1)}\right].\end{split}

(178)

The leading order of
h^{(1)}
is a double pole in
\epsilon
. In order to satisfy eq. (
178
), the leading order of
\zeta^{(2)}
should also be the second order pole in
\epsilon
. With the powers in
\epsilon
made manifest, we have, at the leading order,

\displaystyle\begin{split}0=\zeta^{(1,-2)}+h^{(1,-2)}-\left(h^{(1,-2)}+r^{(1,-2)}\right)\,\quad\Rightarrow\quad\zeta^{(1,-2)}=r^{(1,-2)}\,.\end{split}

(179)

Note that
\frac{1}{2\epsilon}\Gamma_{\mathrm{UV}}^{(1)}
has been replaced by

-\left(h^{(1)}+r^{(1)}\right)
according to eq. (
89
). At
{\cal O}(\epsilon^{-1})
, eq. (
178
) requires

\displaystyle\begin{split}0=\,\,&\zeta^{(1,-1)}+\log\left(\frac{\bar{m}^{2}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mu}}}{}^{2}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mathrm{IR}}}}}}\right)\zeta^{(1,-2)}+h^{(1,-1)}\\
&-\left(h^{(1,-1)}+r^{(1,-1)}\right)+\log\left(\frac{\bar{m}^{2}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.4527,0.132,0.4669}{\mu}}}{}^{2}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.4527,0.132,0.4669}{\mathrm{UV}}}}}}\right)\left(h^{(1,-2)}+r^{(1,-2)}\right)\,.\end{split}

(180)

According to the definition of
h^{(n)}
and
r^{(n)}
(eq. (
79
)), the coefficient of the UV logarithm
\log\left(\frac{\bar{m}^{2}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.4527,0.132,0.4669}{\mu}}}{}^{2}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.4527,0.132,0.4669}{\mathrm{UV}}}}}}\right)
is vanishing. This is because the one-loop correlator
w^{(1)}
in the asymptotic expansion is dominant by a single pole in
\epsilon
, such that
{\cal T}_{\lambda}\left[w^{(2,-2)}\right]=h^{(1,-2)}+r^{(1,-2)}=0
. Therefore, eq. (
185
) implies that

\displaystyle\zeta^{(1,-1)}=r^{(1,-1)}-\log\left(\frac{\bar{m}^{2}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mu}}}{}^{2}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mathrm{IR}}}}}}\right)r^{(1,-2)}\,,

(181)

with IR logarithm
\log\left(\frac{\bar{m}^{2}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mu}}}{}^{2}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mathrm{IR}}}}}}\right)
surviving.

At two loops, the finiteness of eq. (
172
) gives the following constraint,

\displaystyle\begin{split}0=\,\,&\sing\bigg[\left(\frac{\bar{m}^{2}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mu}}}{}^{2}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mathrm{IR}}}}}}\right)^{2\epsilon}\zeta^{(2)}+h^{(2)}+\left(\frac{\bar{m}^{2}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.4527,0.132,0.4669}{\mu}}}{}^{2}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.4527,0.132,0.4669}{\mathrm{UV}}}}}}\right)^{2\epsilon}\frac{1}{4\epsilon}\Gamma_{\mathrm{UV}}^{(2)}\\
&\hskip 20.0pt+\left(\frac{\bar{m}^{2}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mu}}}{}^{2}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mathrm{IR}}}}}}\right)^{2\epsilon}\frac{1}{2}\left(\zeta^{(1)}\right)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\left(h^{(1)}\right)^{2}+\left(\frac{\bar{m}^{2}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.4527,0.132,0.4669}{\mu}}}{}^{2}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.4527,0.132,0.4669}{\mathrm{UV}}}}}}\right)^{2\epsilon}\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{1}{2\epsilon}\Gamma_{\mathrm{UV}}^{(1)}\right)^{2}\\
&\hskip 20.0pt+\,\left(\frac{\bar{m}^{2}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mu}}}{}^{2}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mathrm{IR}}}}}}\right)^{\epsilon}\zeta^{(1)}h^{(1)}+\left(\frac{\bar{m}^{2}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.4527,0.132,0.4669}{\mu}}}{}^{2}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.4527,0.132,0.4669}{\mathrm{UV}}}}}}\right)^{\epsilon}h^{(1)}\frac{1}{2\epsilon}\Gamma_{\mathrm{UV}}^{(1)}+\left(\frac{\bar{m}^{2}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mu}}}{}^{2}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mathrm{IR}}}}}}\right)^{\epsilon}\left(\frac{\bar{m}^{2}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.4527,0.132,0.4669}{\mu}}}{}^{2}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.4527,0.132,0.4669}{\mathrm{UV}}}}}}\right)^{\epsilon}\zeta^{(1)}\frac{1}{2\epsilon}\Gamma_{\mathrm{UV}}^{(1)}\bigg]\,.\end{split}

(182)

The leading order of terms such as
\left(h^{(1)}\right)^{2}
or
\left(\zeta^{(1)}\right)^{2}
, have a quadruple pole in
\epsilon
. The resulting constraint on the quadrupole pole in
\epsilon
is

\displaystyle 0=\zeta^{(2,-4)}+\frac{1}{2}\left[r^{(1,-2)},h^{(1,-2)}\right]+\left(r^{(1,-2)}\right)^{2}-2\zeta^{(1,-2)}r^{(1,-2)}+\left(\zeta^{(1,-2)}\right)^{2}\,.

(183)

However, by plugging in the results given in eqs. (
179
), (
181
), (
176
) and (
89
), one finds that the leading order
\zeta^{(2,-4)}
is vanishing. At the order of the triple pole in
\epsilon
,
r^{(2)}
starts to contribute. Then, eq. (
182
) requires

\displaystyle\zeta^{(2,-3)}=r^{(2,-3)}+\frac{1}{2}\left[h^{(1,-2)},r^{(1,-1)}\right]+\frac{1}{2}\left[h^{(1,-1)},r^{(1,-2)}\right]\,,

(184)

where the one-loop
\zeta^{(1,l)}
has been substituted into
r^{(1,l)}
.
Similarly to the quadrupole case, upon substituting the explicit results we find that
\zeta^{(2,-3)}
is trivial. However, this conclusion relies on cancellation between the two-loop region
r^{(2,-3)}
and the commutators constructed by one-loop functions. This fact
is significant and reflects the effect of the renormalization procedure, not merely a cancellation between one-loop functions as in eq. (
183
).

Starting from the second order pole in
\epsilon
,
\zeta^{(2)}
is non-trivial. We directly give the results of
\zeta^{(2,-2)}
and
\zeta^{(2,-1)}
, skipping some straightforward algebra. We find:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\zeta^{(2,-2)}=\,\,&r^{(2,-2)}+\frac{1}{2}\left[h^{(1,-1)},r^{(1,-1)}\right]+\log\left(\frac{\bar{m}^{2}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mu}}}{}^{2}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mathrm{IR}}}}}}\right)\frac{1}{2}\left[r^{(1,-1)},r^{(1,-2)}\right]\,,\end{split}

(185a)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\zeta^{(2,-1)}=\,\,&r^{(2,-1)}+\frac{1}{2}\left[h^{(1,-1)},r^{(1,0)}\right]+\frac{1}{2}\left[h^{(1,0)},r^{(1,-1)}\right]+\frac{1}{2}\left[r^{(1,-1)},r^{(1,0)}\right]\\
&-\log\left(\frac{\bar{m}^{2}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mu}}}{}^{2}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mathrm{IR}}}}}}\right)\left\{2r^{(2,-2)}+\left[h^{(1,-1)},r^{(1,-1)}\right]\right\}+\log^{2}\left(\frac{\bar{m}^{2}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mu}}}{}^{2}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mathrm{IR}}}}}}\right)\frac{1}{2}\left[r^{(1,-2)},r^{(1,-1)}\right]\,,\end{split}

(185b)

where the commutators that vanish upon substituting the corresponding
h^{(1,l)}
and
r^{(1,l)}
have been omitted. An important feature of eq. (
185
) is that only the IR logarithm appears in
\zeta^{(2)}
. This demonstrates that IR and UV singularities are disentangled. As a result,
\zeta^{(1)}
and
\zeta^{(2)}
are given below,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\zeta^{(1)}=\,\,&\sum_{I}\textbf{T}_{I}\cdot\textbf{T}_{k}\left\{\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\left[\log\left(\frac{(2\beta_{I}\cdot\beta_{k})^{2}}{t_{k}\lambda^{2}\beta_{I}^{2}}\right)-\log\left(\frac{\bar{m}^{2}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mu}}}{}^{2}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mathrm{IR}}}}}}\right)\right]\right\}+\cdots,\end{split}

(186a)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\zeta^{(2)}=\,\,&\sum_{I<J}\mathbf{T}_{IJk}\bigg\{\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}\left[\frac{1-y_{IJk}}{1+y_{IJk}}U_{1}(y_{IJk})+\log\left(y_{IJk}\right)\log\left(\frac{\bar{m}^{2}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mu}}}{}^{2}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mathrm{IR}}}}}}\right)\right]\\
&+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\bigg[\frac{1}{3}\log^{3}(y_{IJk})-\log\left(y_{IJk}\right)\log^{2}\left(\frac{\bar{m}^{2}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mu}}}{}^{2}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mathrm{IR}}}}}}\right)\\
&+\frac{1-y_{IJk}}{1+y_{IJk}}\left(\frac{1}{2}U_{2}(\alpha_{IJ},y_{IJk})-2U_{1}(y_{IJk})\log\left(\frac{\bar{m}^{2}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mu}}}{}^{2}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mathrm{IR}}}}}}\right)\right)\bigg]\bigg\}+\cdots\,,\end{split}

(186b)

where, as above, the ellipsis stand for additional colour structures, such as colour singlet terms at one loop and dipole terms at two loops, which we have not computed.

Next we would like to use the results for
\zeta^{(n)}
for
n=1
and
2
to infer the corresponding mixed-correlator IR anomalous dimension
\Gamma_{\mathrm{IR}}
in eq. (
175
).
We note that both
\zeta^{(1)}
and
\zeta^{(2)}
in eq. (
186
) are dominated by double poles in
\epsilon
. This suggests that there is a single factor of
\log(\tau)
appearing explicitly in
\Gamma_{\mathrm{IR}}
, in addition to the dependence on
\tau
via the running coupling.
Based on this we find that the following simple ansatz for
\Gamma_{\mathrm{IR}}
in eq. (
175
) generates exactly
\zeta^{(1)}
and
\zeta^{(2)}
given in eq. (
186
),

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Gamma_{\mathrm{IR}}(\tau,\alpha_{s}(\tau^{2}))=\,\,&-\sum_{I}\frac{1}{2}\textbf{T}_{I}\cdot\textbf{T}_{k}\gamma_{\text{cusp}}(\alpha_{s})\log\left(\frac{\tau^{2}}{\bar{m}^{2}}\frac{(2\beta_{I}\cdot\beta_{k})^{2}}{t_{k}\lambda^{2}\beta_{I}^{2}}\right)\\
&+\sum_{I<J}\textbf{T}_{IJk}\bigg[\psi_{(IJk)}\left(y_{IJk},\alpha_{s},\frac{\bar{m}^{2}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mu}}}{}^{2}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mathrm{IR}}}}}}\right)\log\left(\frac{\tau^{2}}{\bar{m}^{2}}\right)\\
&+\Psi_{(IJk)}\left(\left\{\alpha_{IJ},y_{IJk}\right\},\alpha_{s},\frac{\bar{m}^{2}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mu}}}{}^{2}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mathrm{IR}}}}}}\right)\bigg]+\cdots\,,\end{split}

(187)

where the leading-order (mixed-correlator) IR ADs for the colour-tripole terms are

\displaystyle\begin{split}\psi_{(IJk)}^{(2)}=\,\,&-8\left[\frac{1-y_{IJk}}{1+y_{IJk}}U_{1}(y_{IJk})+\log\left(y_{IJk}\right)\log\left(\frac{\bar{m}^{2}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mu}}}{}^{2}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mathrm{IR}}}}}}\right)\right],\end{split}

(188a)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Psi_{(IJk)}^{(2)}=\,\,&-4\left[\frac{1-y_{IJk}}{1+y_{IJk}}\frac{1}{2}U_{2}(\alpha_{IJ},y_{IJk})+\frac{1}{3}\log^{3}(y_{IJk})-\log\left(y_{IJk}\right)\log^{2}\left(\frac{\bar{m}^{2}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mu}}}{}^{2}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mathrm{IR}}}}}}\right)\right].\end{split}

(188b)

where, of course, at one loop, tripole terms do not contribute,

\displaystyle\psi_{(IJk)}^{(1)}=\Psi_{(IJk)}^{(1)}=0\,.

(189)

To avoid confusion we stress again that the mixed-correlator IR anomalous dimension, which controls long-distance singularities of a correlator involving an non-regularized lightlike Wilson line, is a very different object to the usual soft anomalous dimension of eq. (
29
), which instead controls long-distance singularities in on-shell amplitudes. The latter corresponds to the UV anomalous dimension of the mixed correlator.
The two anomalous dimensions we determined here control singularities of different origin – IR versus UV – in the mixed correlator itself, and appear respectively on the left and right hand side of the correlator in eq. (
172
).

We also note that in contrast to the UV anomalous dimension, which is independent of the IR regulator
m
, as it must be, the IR anomalous dimension does depend on
m
. This is of course expected, as
Z_{\mathrm{IR}}
is designed to cancel the poles generated by the lightlike line(s), which are not regularized. In particular,
\Gamma_{\mathrm{IR}}
should vanish entirely for a correlator of timelike lines (in a complete regularization scheme) and must be sensitive to changing or removing the regulator where it is active.

Having determined both of the UV and IR anomalous dimensions explicitly in the case of the tripole, it is interesting to compare their analytic structure, which are very different indeed. In particular we find that while the UV anomalous dimension,
\Gamma_{\mathrm{UV}}
, given in eq. (
29
), only contains one rational function,

\frac{1+\alpha_{IJ}^{2}}{1-\alpha_{IJ}^{2}},

(190)

the IR anomalous dimension
\Gamma_{\mathrm{IR}}
contains another,

\frac{1-y_{IJk}}{1+y_{IJk}}\,.

(191)

By Bose symmetry, the kinematic function multiplying the tripole colour structure in eq. (
187
) must be antisymemtric in
(I,J)
and hence, antisymmetric in
y_{IJk}\to 1/y_{IJk}
. In eq. (
188
) this is realised either through the odd powers of the logarithms, or, through the antisymmetry of the rational factor (
191
) in conjunction with the symmetry of the corresponding transcendental functions
U_{i}
.
Further note that the denominators of the two rational factors in eqs. (
190
) and (
191
), also appear as the symbol letters in the transcendental functions which multiply them, such as
U_{2}(\alpha_{IJ},y_{IJk})

and
M_{100}(\alpha_{IJ})
of eqs. (
208b
)
and (
206
), respectively.
Note that, as often happens, the transcendental functions multiplying the rational factor (
191
) are power suppressed near
y_{IJk}=-1
,

\displaystyle U_{1}(y_{IJk})\sim U_{2}(\alpha_{IJ},y_{IJk})\sim{\cal O}(1+y_{IJk})\,,

(192)

so the pole at
y_{IJk}=-1
is spurious and the function
\Gamma_{\mathrm{IR}}
is regular in the limit
y_{IJk}\to-1
. This is similar to what happens in the
\alpha_{IJ}=1
limit of eqs. (
13b
) and (
29
). Both situations correspond to the straight-line limit, where lines
I
and
J
are in the same direction, but one is in the initial state and the other in the final state. This special limit was used to set the boundary condition for the connected tripole web
in appendix
D.3.1
, following eq. (
280
) there.

To write down the full expression of
\Gamma_{\mathrm{IR}}
, we also have to consider configurations involving only lightlike Wilson lines,
such as lightlike dipoles and singlets which are hidden in the ellipsis in eq. (
186
). Fortunately, with the exponential regulator defined in eq. (
34
), purely lightlike webs of the mixed correlator are scaleless, where IR and UV singularities exactly cancel each other out. Therefore, the corresponding terms appearing in
\Gamma_{\mathrm{IR}}
should be exactly the same as those in
\Gamma_{\mathrm{UV}}
, given in eq. (
30
). Therefore, the IR anomalous dimension
\Gamma_{\mathrm{IR}}
is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Gamma_{\mathrm{IR}}(\tau,\alpha_{s}(\tau^{2}))=\,\,&\sum_{i}\tilde{\gamma}_{i}(\alpha_{s})-\sum_{i}\sum_{I}\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{T}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{T}_{I}\gamma_{\text{cusp}}\left(\alpha_{s}\right)\log\left(\frac{\tau^{2}}{\bar{m}^{2}}\frac{(2\beta_{I}\cdot\beta_{i})^{2}}{t_{i}\lambda^{2}\beta_{I}^{2}}\right)\\
&-\sum_{i<j}\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{T}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{T}_{j}\gamma_{\text{cusp}}\left(\alpha_{s}\right)\log\left(\frac{(2\beta_{i}\cdot\beta_{j})^{2}}{t_{i}t_{j}\lambda^{4}}\right)\\
&+\sum_{I<J}\sum_{k}\textbf{T}_{IJk}\bigg[\psi_{(IJk)}\left(y_{IJk},\alpha_{s},\frac{\bar{m}^{2}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mu}}}{}^{2}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mathrm{IR}}}}}}\right)\log\left(\frac{\tau^{2}}{\bar{m}^{2}}\right)\\
&+\Psi_{(IJk)}\left(\left\{\alpha_{IJ},y_{IJk}\right\},\alpha_{s},\frac{\bar{m}^{2}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mu}}}{}^{2}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mathrm{IR}}}}}}\right)\bigg].\end{split}

(193)

Note that the colour structures free a lightlike index, for example
\mathbf{T}_{IJK}
, do not appear in
\Gamma_{\mathrm{IR}}
, since webs involving only timelike lines
are fully regularized in the IR and thus only generate UV singularities.

We managed to obtain the renormalization factors
Z_{\mathrm{IR}}
and
Z_{\mathrm{UV}}
for the mixed correlator. However, one may not be entirely satisfied with the
Z_{\mathrm{IR}}
and
Z_{\mathrm{UV}}
presented in eqs. (
193
) and (
30
), because the mixed correlator itself has no
\lambda
dependence: the strict limit is well-defined, having no memory of the expansion parameter.
Apparently, our procedure of determining the separate renormalization factors
\Gamma_{\mathrm{IR}}
and
\Gamma_{\mathrm{UV}}
, has led to both being dependent on
t_{k}\lambda^{2}
. One should therefore expect that this dependence can be removed.
In fact, we will see that the
\lambda
dependence in eqs. (
187
) and (
30
) can be regarded as the ambiguity in separating the IR and UV singularities for colour dipole terms.

To demonstrate this and completely remove the
\lambda
dependence from both the UV and the IR anomalous dimensions, we now identify both renormalization scales as the regulator scale itself, that is
{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mu_{\mathrm{IR}}}}={\color[rgb]{0.4527,0.132,0.4669}{\mu_{\mathrm{UV}}}}=\bar{m}
. In this case the logarithms depending on the ratio scales in eq. (
186
) vanish
17
17

17

The dependence on the regulator
\bar{m}
in the IR AD persists via the terms proportional to
\psi_{(IJk)}
and
\gamma_{\text{cusp}}
, see eq. (
187
)), which generate higher-order poles through the integration in eq. (
173b
).
and these scales are eliminated from
\zeta^{(i)}
and thus from the factor
Z_{\mathrm{IR}}
.
With this, let us now prove that the
\lambda
dependence can be eliminated, while recovering the correct dependence on the dimensionful momenta.
To this end we introduce the following exponential,

\displaystyle Z_{0}(a,b)\equiv\text{P}\exp\left[\int_{a}^{b}\frac{d\tau}{\tau}\Gamma_{0}(\tau,\alpha_{s}(\tau))\right],

(194)

where
\Gamma_{0}
contains only dipole terms and takes the general form

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Gamma_{0}(\tau,\alpha_{s}(\tau))\equiv\,\,&\sum_{i}\sum_{I}\mathbf{T}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{T}_{I}\Gamma_{i}+\sum_{i<j}\mathbf{T}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{T}_{j}\left(\Gamma_{i}+\Gamma_{j}\right).\end{split}

(195)

Notice that in the first term on the right-hand side of eq. (
195
),
\Gamma_{i}
is independent of
I
, that is, it is the same common factor for all timelike Wilson lines
I
.

We now show that upon taking colour conservation into account, the dipole terms taking the form of eq. (
195
) can be inserted into
\Gamma_{\mathrm{IR}}
and
\Gamma_{\mathrm{UV}}
simultaneously without changing the result. Therefore,
Z_{0}
encapsulates the freedom on shifting terms of the form of
\Gamma_{0}
between the IR and the UV factors in eq. (
172
).
Note that the
\lambda
dependence of
\Gamma_{\mathrm{IR}}
and
\Gamma_{\mathrm{UV}}
in eqs. (
193
) and (
30
) exactly satisfy the form of
\Gamma_{0}
. Importantly they are all proportional to
\gamma_{\text{cusp}}
.
Additionally, recall that the neutral regions at one loop,
{\cal R}_{(Jk)}^{\{C_{N}\}}
and
{\cal R}_{(Ik)}^{\{C_{N}\}}
, are constants given in eq. (
219
).
They contribute to
\zeta^{(1)}
and hence to the dipole terms in
\Gamma_{\mathrm{IR}}
. These neutral collinear contributions may also be understood as representing the ambiguity in separating between UV and IR singularities.
18
18

18

In section
4
, we assumed that there is only one lightlike line
k
with an arbitrary number of timelike lines (at least two). In that case only the first term on the right-hand side of eq. (
195
) survives. Then, the one-loop neutral regions,
{\cal R}_{(Jk)}^{\{C_{N}\}}
and
{\cal R}_{(Ik)}^{\{C_{N}\}}
, match exactly the form of
\Gamma_{0}
. We can interpret these regions as either IR or UV singularities. In the present section, we generalize the setup to any number of lightlike lines, such that the second term in eq. (
195
) also contributes.

To proceed with the proof, we collect the coefficient of
\Gamma_{i}
on the right-hand side of eq. (
195
),

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Gamma_{0}=\,\,&\sum_{i}\mathbf{T}_{i}\cdot\left(\sum_{j\neq i}\mathbf{T}_{j}+\sum_{I}\mathbf{T}_{I}\right)\Gamma_{i}=-\sum_{i}C_{i}\mathbf{1}\Gamma_{i}\,.\end{split}

(196)

At the second equal sign, we use colour conservation and
\Gamma_{0}
becomes proportional to the identity
\mathbf{1}
in colour space,
times
C_{i}
, the quadratic Casimir of line
i
.
Additionally, by setting
a
and
b
in eq. (
194
) to be
0
and
\infty
respectively, the exponent of
Z_{0}(a,b)
is scaleless,

\displaystyle Z_{0}(0,\infty)=\text{P}\exp\left[\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{d\tau}{\tau}\Gamma_{0}\right]=\mathbf{1}.

(197)

Therefore, we have the freedom to insert
Z_{0}
in the right-hand side of eq. (
172
) at no cost. We then split
Z_{0}
into IR and UV parts,

\displaystyle Z_{0}(0,\infty)=Z_{0}(0,\bar{m})Z_{0}(\bar{m},\infty)=\mathbf{1}.

(198)

Note that the order of
Z_{0}(0,\bar{m})
and
Z_{0}(\bar{m},\infty)
is arbitrary because
\Gamma_{0}
is proportional to colour identity; see eq. (
196
). We then absorb
Z_{0}(0,\bar{m})
and
Z_{0}(\bar{m},\infty)
into
\Gamma_{\mathrm{IR}}
and
\Gamma_{\mathrm{UV}}
, respectively,

\displaystyle\begin{split}Z_{\mathrm{UV}}\rightarrow\bar{Z}_{\mathrm{UV}}\equiv Z_{0}(0,\bar{m})Z_{\mathrm{UV}},\end{split}

(199a)

\displaystyle\begin{split}Z_{\mathrm{IR}}\rightarrow\bar{Z}_{\mathrm{IR}}\equiv Z_{0}(\bar{m},\infty)Z_{\mathrm{IR}},\end{split}

(199b)

while the ADs become,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Gamma_{\mathrm{UV}}\rightarrow\bar{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{UV}}\equiv\Gamma_{\mathrm{UV}}+\Gamma_{0},\end{split}

(200a)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Gamma_{\mathrm{IR}}\rightarrow\bar{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{IR}}\equiv\Gamma_{\mathrm{IR}}+\Gamma_{0}\,.\end{split}

(200b)

Because
\Gamma_{0}
does not contain any terms that involve only timelike lines, we can
interpret
\Gamma_{0}
as the ambiguity in distinguishing between IR and UV singularities for massless lines.
In particular, individual terms in
\Gamma_{0}
involving
\Gamma_{i}
, carry the information on how the UV versus IR separation is made for line
i
.
By making a special choice of
\Gamma_{i}
such that

\displaystyle\Gamma_{i}=-\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{\text{cusp}}(\alpha_{s})\log\left(\frac{\bar{m}^{2}}{\tau^{2}}t_{i}\lambda^{2}\right),

(201)

the
\lambda
dependence in both eq. (
30
) and eq. (
187
) are cancelled.
The
\lambda
-independent ADs are then

\displaystyle\begin{split}\\
\bar{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{UV}}(\tau,\alpha_{s}(\tau^{2}))=\,\,&\sum_{i}\tilde{\gamma}_{i}(\alpha_{s})+\sum_{I}\Omega_{(I)}(\alpha_{s})+\sum_{I<J}\mathbf{T}_{I}\cdot\mathbf{T}_{J}\Omega_{(IJ)}(\alpha_{IJ},\alpha_{s})\\
&+\sum_{i<j}\mathbf{T}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{T}_{j}\gamma_{\text{cusp}}(\alpha_{s})\log\frac{\tau^{2}}{-2p_{i}\cdot p_{j}}\\
&+\sum_{i}\sum_{I}\mathbf{T}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{T}_{I}\gamma_{\text{cusp}}(\alpha_{s})\log\frac{\tau\sqrt{p_{I}^{2}}}{-2p_{i}\cdot p_{I}}\\
&+\sum_{I<J<K}\textbf{T}_{IJK}\Omega_{(IJK)}(\{\alpha_{IJ},\alpha_{JK},\alpha_{IK}\},\alpha_{s})\\
&+\sum_{I<J}\sum_{k}\textbf{T}_{IJk}\Omega_{(IJk)}\left(\left\{\alpha_{IJ},y_{IJk}\right\},\alpha_{s}\right)\\
=\,\,&\sum_{i}\tilde{\gamma}_{i}(\alpha_{s})+\Gamma^{T}+\Gamma^{L},\end{split}

(202a)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\bar{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{IR}}(\tau,\alpha_{s}(\tau^{2}))=\,\,&\sum_{i}\tilde{\gamma}_{i}(\alpha_{s})-\sum_{i}\sum_{I}\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{T}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{T}_{I}\gamma_{\text{cusp}}\left(\alpha_{s}\right)\log\left(\frac{(2\beta_{I}\cdot\beta_{i})^{2}}{\beta_{I}^{2}}\right)\\
&+\sum_{i<j}\mathbf{T}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{T}_{j}\gamma_{\text{cusp}}(\alpha_{s})\log\frac{\tau^{2}}{-2p_{i}\cdot p_{j}}\\
&+\sum_{I<J}\sum_{k}\textbf{T}_{IJk}\bigg[\psi_{(IJk)}(y_{IJk},\alpha_{s})\log\left(\frac{\tau^{2}}{\bar{m}^{2}}\right)\\
&+\Psi_{(IJk)}\left(\left\{\alpha_{IJ},y_{IJk}\right\},\alpha_{s}\right)\bigg],\end{split}

(202b)

where we have recovered the dimensionful momentum
p_{i}
defined by

\displaystyle p_{i}^{\nu}\equiv\bar{m}\beta_{i}^{\nu}\,,

(203)

defining the normalization of the dimensionless velocity
\beta_{i}
for lightlike lines. Notice that, in contrast to eq. (
193
), the tripole terms in
\bar{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{IR}}
do not depend on
\bar{m}/{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mu_{\mathrm{IR}}}}
, since
{\color[rgb]{0.8921,0,0.1125}{\mu_{\mathrm{IR}}}}
has been chosen to be
\bar{m}
, and we have,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\psi_{(IJk)}^{(2)}\left(y_{IJk},\alpha_{s}\right)=\,\,&-8\frac{1-y_{IJk}}{1+y_{IJk}}U_{1}(y_{IJk}),\end{split}

(204a)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Psi_{(IJk)}^{(2)}\left(\left\{\alpha_{IJ},y_{IJk}\right\},\alpha_{s}\right)=\,\,&-4\left[\frac{1-y_{IJk}}{1+y_{IJk}}\frac{1}{2}U_{2}(\alpha_{IJ},y_{IJk})+\frac{1}{3}\log^{3}(y_{IJk})\right].\end{split}

(204b)

The ADs,
\Gamma^{T}
and
\Gamma^{L}
, in the second line of eq. (
202a
), are defined in eqs. (
7
) and (
8
), respectively. The colour singlet term
\tilde{\gamma_{i}}
is the same as that in
\Gamma_{\mathrm{UV}}
in eq. (
30
).
Notice that with the special choice of
\Gamma_{i}
made in eq. (
201
), the UV AD
\bar{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{UV}}
in eq. (
202a
) is exactly the soft AD of on-shell amplitudes, up to the colour singlet term.
19
19

19

In the soft AD of the amplitudes, the colour singlet term
\gamma_{i}
is known as the collinear AD. To obtain the correct collinear AD, partonic hard collinear singularities should also be considered, which is not discussed in this paper.

Finally, the renormalization of the mixed correlator becomes

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left<\phi_{\beta_{1}}\cdots\phi_{\beta_{N}}\Phi^{(m)}_{\beta_{N+1}}\cdots\Phi^{(m)}_{\beta_{N+M}}\right>_{\text{ren.}}=\bar{Z}_{\mathrm{IR}}(\bar{m})\left<\phi_{\beta_{1}}\cdots\phi_{\beta_{N}}\Phi^{(m)}_{\beta_{N+1}}\cdots\Phi^{(m)}_{\beta_{N+M}}\right>\bar{Z}_{\mathrm{UV}}(\bar{m}).\end{split}

(205)

The structure we have here is invariant with respect to the way in which the lightlike limit is taken, i.e., there is no dependence on
\lambda
in either factor.
At the same time, we lose the freedom to choose a
t_{k}
as we did in eq. (
62
), such that the limit preserves rescaling symmetry. We emphasize that
\bar{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{UV}}
captures soft singularities of on-shell amplitudes, while
\bar{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{IR}}
describes special IR singularities of the mixed correlator and does not directly appear in physical processes. Understanding the renormalization structure of the mixed correlator itself, is an important step forward, as this object provides a new route towards efficient computation of
\bar{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{UV}}
at higher loop orders.

6
Conclusions

In this paper we presented a new method to compute the soft anomalous dimension controlling long-distance singularities of amplitudes with both massive and massless particles. As reviewed in section
2
, the standard approach to this problem is based on the computation of correlators of fully IR-regularized,
timelike
semi-infinite Wilson lines, which are subsequently expanded near the lightcone, so as to place the massless particles on shell.
A key example of such a computation is the determination of the three-loop soft anomalous dimension for massless scattering
Almelid:2015jia
. In this case configuration-space integrals contributing to the correlator of four timelike lines were performed to derive multifold Mellin-Barnes representations, which were subsequently used to take the simultaneous lightlike limit of all Wilson lines, at which point major simplifications took place, and the remaining integrals could be performed. Such a computation may be hard to complete if the limit of interest is more complicated, e.g. if only a subset of the Wilson lines become lightlike.
The reason for taking all Wilson lines to be non-lightlike in the first place is rather fundamental: it guarantees multiplicative renormalizability of the correlator, making it possible to deduce the soft anomalous dimension from the single UV
1/\epsilon
pole of the correlator.
Of course, this advantage comes at the a high price in terms of the increased complexity of the integrals. One may wonder whether there is a strategy that makes better use of the simpler nature of the lightlike limit at the outset. This our starting point in this paper.

Our approach is based on replacing said correlator by its asymptotic expansion near the lightcone, using the method of regions. This gives rise, in particular, to a “hard region” integral, where the strict lightlike limit is taken prior to the loop-momentum integration.
These hard-region integrals are precisely those defined by a correlator consisting of both timelike and lightlike lines, so that each external particle is represented by a Wilson line along its exact classical trajectory. The computation of this object has been avoided in the past because its singularity structure is complex and is hard to interpret. In particular, it is not multiplicatively renormalizable in the usual sense. Moreover, in contrast the case of timelike Wilson lines, such a correlator cannot be fully regularized in the IR without breaking gauge invariance and rescaling symmetry. In the absence of complete regularization of long-distance and collinear singularities, higher-order poles in
\epsilon
are generated, obscuring the connection between the correlator and the sought-after anomalous dimension.

In this paper we solved this problem, outlining a general strategy for determining the soft AD from the sum of all region integrals, see eqs. (
89
) and (
156
) at one and two loops, respectively.
Moreover, we have shown how the UV and IR singularities can be systematically disentangled.
As shown in eq. (
172
), from the perspective of the mixed correlator, the sought-after soft AD still corresponds to UV renormalization (
173a
), except that it is now recovered through an asymptotic expansion. Of course, the UV renormalization of the mixed correlator
does not remove all its singularities – the remaining ones can be identified as of IR origin, defining
\Gamma_{\mathrm{IR}}

in eq. (
173b
).
The latter is a very different object, which depends on the way the correlator is regularized in the IR.
We have seen that the classification of singularities based on their non-analytic scaling,
\lambda^{n_{R}\epsilon}
, with positive versus negative
n_{R}
within the MoR, nicely translates into the separate
Z_{\mathrm{UV}}
and
Z_{\mathrm{IR}}
factors in eq. (
172
), respectively. Interestingly, neutral regions other than the hard, have a unique role as, owing to colour conservation, they describe the inherent ambiguity in assigning dipole contributions obtained using the MoR to either of the two anomalous dimensions. This was used in eq. (
202
) to eliminate the freedom in choosing the MoR expansion parameter, and restore the proper dependence of the ADs on dimensionful momenta.

In this paper we demonstrated our strategy at two loops, where the key example has been the correlator of three Wilson lines, one of which becomes lightlike. Specifically, the fully-antisymmetric contribution to the soft AD proportional to the tripole

\textbf{T}_{IJK}
(see eq. (
12
)), served us as case study.
In contrast to dipole terms, the lightlike limit of the tripole soft AD is smooth, tending to a finite limit, see eq. (
29
). Such a finite lightlike limit is expected to arise also in more complicated higher-order contributions to the soft AD, in fact, in any contribution depending on rescaling-invariant kinematic variables.
It is useful to note that the finite limit of the soft ADs does not simply translate into a finite limit of the corresponding correlator term. Indeed, the two-loops correlator in eq. (
151
) is still logarithmically-divergent in the expansion parameter
\lambda
. These logarithms are associated with UV subdivergences generated by sequentially contracting individual gluon exchanges to the multi-Wilson-line vertex. They are therefore removed by commutators of one-loop webs when computing the soft AD, see eq. (
156
). This subtraction has the very same effect to the definition of subtracted webs
Gardi:2013saa
.

In section
4
we performed a detailed MoR-based analysis of one-loop and two-loop webs, with a special focus on the contributions to the tripole colour structure, originally computed in refs.
Mitov:2009sv
;
Ferroglia:2009ii
;
Ferroglia:2009ep
.
For each web we verified that the asymptotic expansion obtained through a sum of all region integrals correctly reproduces the leading term in the lightcone expansion,
\beta_{K}^{2}\to 0
, of the corresponding web computed with all timelike lines. Beyond providing assurance for the validity of the MoR in this, somewhat unorthodox application, our central aim in this analysis was to understand the modes and the regions which arise, and then further investigate how these contribute to the correlator, and hence to the anomalous dimension. Working in Euclidean kinematics, where all
\alpha_{IJ}>0
, there are only endpoint singularities in parameters space, and then the geometric MoR construction is guaranteed to provide the complete set of regions. Starting with the set of region vectors computed by
pySecDec
(defined in the Lee-Pomeransky representation) we determined the set of loop-momentum modes and regions.
All the regions are summarized in tables
1
,
4
, and
3
, where they are expressed in terms of the five modes defined in table
2
. These include a single IR-collinear mode,
C_{\mathrm{IR}}
, two neutral modes, the hard,
H
, and the neutral-collinear mode,
C_{N}
, and two UV modes, UV-collinear,
C_{\mathrm{UV}}
, and
UV-hard,
H_{\mathrm{UV}}
. The latter two appear starting from two loops.

We observe that the set of modes and regions is similar in different webs.
It is therefore insightful to define

region functions
, (see eq. (
58
)) which sum up the contributions to the correlator from different webs, which share a common region
R
as well as a common colour factor.
Furthermore, we define
invariant region functions in the final line of eq. (
58
), which sum up all region functions with a common overall asymptotic behaviour,
\lambda^{n_{R}\epsilon}
, but different set of modes.
In terms of these invariant region functions we find a remarkably simple picture. First, all UV regions completely cancel out at correlator level. Second, neutral regions other than the hard only contribute a constant to the dipole structure, as mentioned above.
Thus, the tripole structure of the correlator receives only two types of contributions, the hard region and the IR regions, summarized in eqs. (
149
) and (
150
), respectively. The UV singularities of the correlator,
Z_{\mathrm{UV}}
in (
172
), from which the soft AD is determined, are then recovered in the sum of all regions, while the remaining singularities, corresponding to
Z_{\mathrm{IR}}
, are driven by the IR regions according to eqs. (
175
) and (
185
).

In contrast to the IR modes, which are common in on-shell expansions, the presence of multiple neutral modes with hard virtuality, which arise in our lightcone expansion, is unique to correlators of semi-infinite Wilson lines.
In section
4.3
showed that under a certain Lorentz boost, the two neutral modes,
H
and
C_{N}
, behave respectively as the hard mode in two different Lorentz frames; see eq. (
167
).
Moreover, in terms of the Wilson-line velocities, these two hard modes correspond to two physically-distinct limits, the lightcone limit of eq. (
157
) and the complementary lightcone limit of eq. (
161
).
While in the lightcone expansion one particle is becoming massless, in the complementary one, all other particles become massless and collinear to each other.
A key observation is that the two limits share the same mathematical result due to the rescaling symmetry of the Wilson lines. As explained following eq. (
167
), this is a generalization of the identification between the massless limit and the triple collinear limit discussed in refs.
Duhr:2025cye
;
GZ-PRL
;
GZ-TBP
.
Another conclusion from this analysis is that the observed degeneracy of the neutral modes stems from the rescaling symmetry of the correlator of semi-infinite Wilson lines.

While in this paper we developed and tested the approach using well-known one- and two-loop examples, its generalization
to higher loop orders with arbitrary numbers of lightlike and timelike Wilson lines is in principle straightforward.
Indeed, we have been able to apply this method to evaluate the correlator of three lightlike lines and one timelike line at three-loop order, and determine the function multiplying the quadrupole colour structure. This result, along with earlier work by Liu and Schalch
Liu:2022elt
, completes the knowledge of the soft AD for the scattering of a single massive particle and any number of massless ones at three loops. Details of this three-loop calculation and the results for the soft AD will soon be published
GZ-PRL
;
GZ-TBP
.

.
